**Corrections from implementing Task 3** (landed as `f7cae52` and
`0e3bbb2`; every mutation below was applied to the shipped code and
watched fail the test that claims it, then restored, with the baseline
confirmed green in between). Six findings. Three of them are places this
task's own code block and its own test block disagreed, and the test was
right each time.

1. **`builtinNames` sorted alphabetically contradicted this task's own
   test.** The block built the printed vocabulary with `sort.Strings`,
   which yields `@created_at, @invalid, @key, @name, @type`, while
   `TestAFieldKeyIsAFieldKeyOrASigil` asserts the refusal message reads
   `the built-ins are @name, @key, @type, @invalid, @created_at`. The
   list ships in **declaration order**, which is the better answer on its
   own merits — `@name` is the one an agent reaches for, and the list is
   read as a vocabulary rather than scanned as an index. `sort.Strings`
   survives only in `allOperatorNames`, where the opposite is true: that
   list *is* scanned for a spelling.
   The two derived structures — `builtinTypes` and `builtinNames` — are
   now built from one ordered `builtins` slice rather than a map plus a
   sort, so a built-in that is printed but has no declared type (and so
   would admit no operator at all) cannot exist.
   `TestTheBuiltinVocabularyIsListedInTheOrderItIsDeclared` pins both
   halves and the order.

2. **The depth refusal's pointer contradicted this task's own test too.**
   `checkPredicateAt` reported the depth bound at the node that tripped
   it, so a nine-deep `not` would answer at
   `/from/0/where/not/not/not/not/not/not/not/not`, while
   `TestAPredicateTreeIsBounded` asserts `/from/0/where`. The test is
   right: "this tree is too deep" and "this tree has too many
   conditions" are statements about the *whole predicate*, and a pointer
   forty segments long names no mistake the caller made. Both tree bounds
   now report at the predicate's own root, which is threaded through the
   walk as `predicateWalk.root`.

3. **The node cap would have answered with one copy of its sentence per
   sibling past it.** The block returned early from the offending subtree
   but let the loop continue, so a 500-condition `all` reported the same
   message three hundred times. `predicateWalk` carries a `tooDeep` and a
   `tooMany` flag and says each thing once.
   `TestAPredicateTreeIsBoundedByItsNodeCount` asserts the count is
   exactly one, which is what a mutation removing the flag turns red.

4. **Step 4's correction was applied without shipping step 3's draft
   first.** The plan writes the `all`/`any` double-walk as a deliberate
   defect for a reviewer to find. Committing knowingly-wrong code to
   perform a review round is a worse trade than writing it right: the
   recursion ships as `predicateWalk.children`, addressing each child by
   its index under the combinator's own name, once.

5. **The decision this task was asked to make: a predicate value gets its
   own depth bound, `MaxValueDepth = 2`.** Task 2's review recorded that
   `MaxPredicateDepth` bounds the predicate *tree* and never looks inside
   a `value`, leaving `encoding/json`'s own limit of ten thousand as the
   only bound. Two is the deepest a *legal* value goes — a scalar is 1, a
   list of scalars is 2, a `{"param": "…"}` reference is 2 — so nothing
   legal is refused. The argument for bounding it at all is not stack
   safety (the decoder's limit already means nothing overflows) but the
   three things the decoder's limit does not do: it reports at pointer
   `""` in the decoder's own wording, saying nothing about a value; the
   shape checks cannot cover the gap, because a list operator counts its
   list and never looks inside it, so `{"op":"in","value":[[["a"]]]}`
   passes every check this task otherwise makes; and a query document is
   *stored* — an unbounded blob in `value` becomes a saved view that
   every later stage re-walks. `TestAPredicateValueHasItsOwnDepthBound`
   pins the refusal, the list-element case the shape checks miss, and the
   two legal shapes that sit exactly on the bound.

6. **"The table is data rather than a switch" was pinned by nothing, and
   `shapeOf` was where it would have failed.** `operatorsByType` being
   data buys nothing on its own: `shapeOf` is a second table over the
   same keys, and a missing entry reads as `shapeScalar` — the zero value
   — so an operator added to a type's row without a shape would let `in`
   take a bare number and reach the compiler as one, silently.
   `TestAnOperatorCannotBeHalfAdded` asserts the domain of `shapeOf` is
   exactly `knownOperators`, **in both directions**, and that the printed
   vocabulary covers the same set.
   `TestEveryOperatorNameIsEitherKnownOrNamedBack` then drives every
   spelling in the table through `ParseQuery` with the value shape it
   declares, so an operator in the table that the structural pass refuses
   is red too.

**Two things added beyond the block, each closing something a comment
claimed and nothing pinned:**

- `TestEveryProjectionAttributeIsChecked` drives all five of a
  projection's attribute references through `ParseQuery`. `checkProjection`
  is the one place in this task that enumerates members rather than
  walking them — five named positions with five different pointers — and
  a sixth reference added to `Projection` without a line in
  `projectionAttrs` would reach the compiler unchecked. The five now sit
  in an ordered `projectionAttrs` slice rather than a map, so the pass is
  deterministic as well as covered.
- `TestNoDocumentTypeCarriesCallerTextInAByteSlice` verifies rather than
  assumes the promise this task inherited. `walkStrings` skips a plain
  `[]byte` deliberately — its bytes decode from base64 and are not a
  string the caller wrote — and its comment says a document type must
  not use one for caller text. Nothing pinned that: such a field would be
  caller text the bound never sees, and the walk would report nothing
  rather than fail. The test walks the `reflect.Type` graph reachable
  from `Query`, so it covers shapes no test document populates, which is
  what makes it a guard for the fourteen later tasks that add types under
  this walk. It also refuses a `chan` or `func` member, which the walk
  ignores in the same silence.

**Recorded for Task 4**, verified rather than assumed:

- **Nothing this task added goes under the text walk.** `ParamRef`,
  `valueShape`, `predicateWalk` and `projectionAttrs` are all
  unreachable from `Query`. The four document types (`Predicate`,
  `FieldRef`, `AttrRef`, `RelHop`) **stayed in `query.go`**, where Task 2
  declared them, and `predicate.go` declares no document type at all —
  an earlier draft of this sentence said they had moved, which they had
  not. What the sentence was supporting is true either way and was
  verified independently: `TestEveryStringInAQueryIsBounded` still counts
  exactly twenty-seven positions, and the type-graph test above is what
  will catch the next addition.
- **`BuiltinType(name)` is exported for Task 4.** A built-in's declared
  type is what `AdmitsOperator` has to be called with, and Task 4 needs
  the same answer this package's own checks need — a second copy of the
  table in `resolve.go` is exactly the drift `internal/paging`'s package
  comment exists to prevent.
- **`checkLimits` runs after the structural pass and returns on the first
  code it raises**, so a document that is both malformed and over-cap
  answers `query_invalid` and never mentions the limit. That is
  deliberate — `limit_exceeded`'s advice is "lower this number", which is
  only actionable on a document that is otherwise well formed — and it
  means Task 4 must not assume a single response carries both codes.
- **The `Default*` limit constants are declared and unread.**
  `DefaultMaxDepth`, `DefaultMaxNodes` and `DefaultMaxEdges` ship with
  the caps because they are one table and splitting it would drift, but
  nothing applies them yet: Task 6's compiler is what reads a limit that
  the document left unset.

**Corrections from reviewing Task 3** (landed as `ee3ab89` and
`5639dce`; every mutation named below was applied to the shipped code,
watched fail the test that claims it, and restored, with the baseline
confirmed green in between).

7. **A guard this task's own comment claimed did not exist, at the spot
   Task 9 will read.** `projectionAttrs`' comment said
   `TestEveryProjectionAttributeIsChecked` makes "a sixth reference added
   to `Projection` without a line here fail a test rather than reaching
   the compiler unchecked". That test iterates a **hardcoded list of five
   names**, which proves the five that exist are checked and nothing
   about a sixth: adding `ShapeBy *AttrRef` to `Projection` with no line
   in `projectionAttrs` left the entire suite green, and
   `{"project":{"shape_by":"@nmae"}}` parsed with a misspelled built-in
   intact. A list of names cannot be the guard against a name nobody has
   written yet.
   `TestEveryProjectionAttributeReferenceHasALineInTheTable` asks
   `Projection` itself, over `reflect.Type`, which of its members are
   `*AttrRef`, and refuses one the table does not name — in the style
   `TestNoDocumentTypeCarriesCallerTextInAByteSlice` already uses. The
   same mutation is now red, naming the member and the line to add.

8. **The same hole existed in the two other enumerate-don't-walk
   positions, and is closed the same way.** This is the third such
   position in the package, and Tasks 7, 8 and 12 all add members to
   those types.
   `TestEveryLimitInTheDocumentIsJudged` reads the `*int` members off
   `Limits` and drives each through `ParseQuery`, so a fourth limit with
   no line in `checkLimits` — a bound the engine advertises and never
   applies — is red. `TestEveryPredicateInAStepIsChecked` reads the
   `*Predicate` members off `Step` and drives each with a field key no
   grammar admits, so a third tree with no `checkPredicate` call — an
   unbounded, unvalidated tree stored in a saved view — is red. Adding
   `MaxHops *int` and `NodeWhere *Predicate` respectively was green
   before and is red now.

9. **The type-graph test's sanity list named nine of thirteen.** The
   guard itself was always whole — it fires on whatever the walk touches
   — but the "did the walk reach anything" half omitted `ParamDecl`,
   `NodeSet`, `EdgeSpec` and `Depth`, so if `Params`, `Nodes` or `Edges`
   stopped being reachable the test would still have passed over a graph
   it no longer walked. All thirteen are named, and the count is asserted
   as well, so a type *added* to the document without a line is caught
   too. Making `ParamDecl` unreachable is now red.

10. **`params[].default` was unbounded in depth.** The storage argument
    for `MaxValueDepth` applies to it verbatim: a default is an `any`
    like a predicate's `value`, `ParseQuery` is what stands between a
    document and the row that stores it, and a 401-deep default parsed.
    Task 4's coercion will refuse a non-scalar, but the *structural*
    bound is this task's, and a document is stored before Task 4 runs.
    `checkQuery` now judges a default against `MaxValueDepth` at
    `/params/N/default`, which refuses nothing legal — a default is one
    scalar of one of the three scalar types.
    `TestAParameterDefaultIsBoundedLikeAValue` pins the refusal and the
    four legal declarations, including the omitted default.

11. **Four smaller things nothing held.**
    - **Three collection caps had a refusal test at cap+1 and no
      acceptance test at the cap**, so narrowing `>` to `>=` would have
      taken one value, one condition or one character off the language
      with nothing noticing. The value-list cap, the predicate-node cap
      and the field-key length now each have that control, which the
      depth bounds already had. All three narrowings are red.
    - **The three field-key length checks outside a predicate's own
      `field` were unpinned** — the scalar spelling of an `AttrRef`, a
      one-hop `related.attr`, and a key in `project.fields` — and all
      three survived removal, leaving a 4096-character key legal in that
      position alone. `TestEveryFieldKeyLengthIsBoundedWhereverOneIsWritten`
      covers all three, refusal and acceptance.
    - **`valueDepth`'s object arm was unpinned.**
      `TestAPredicateValueHasItsOwnDepthBound` drives lists inside lists
      only, while the correction above advertises the `{"param": …}`
      object case; deleting the map arm was green.
      `TestAValueIsBoundedThroughObjectsAsWellAsLists` drives the three
      mixed nestings through `ParseQuery` and the arithmetic of both arms
      as a unit table. Deleting the map arm is red.
    - **`BuiltinType` is exported for Task 4 with no sigil invariant.**
      A sigil-less entry in `builtins` would be found by a lookup for a
      *declared* field of that name and would shadow it, silently reading
      a game's own field as a built-in.
      `TestEveryBuiltinCarriesItsSigil` asserts the sigil, a declared
      type, and that the exported answer is the table's own; adding
      `{"level", metamodel.FieldNumber}` is red.

**Recorded for Task 11**, which stores what `ParseQuery` returns:
`ParseQuery`'s doc comment now states what a parsed query is and is not,
because Task 11 is the caller that has to know. **Structurally it is
safe to store**: every string is length-bounded, every collection is
count-bounded, and every tree and every `any` is depth-bounded —
including `params[].default`, as of correction 10 above; before it, a
parsed query was *not* safe to store, and that is the shape of the gap
worth remembering. **Semantically it is unjudged**: a default is not yet
known to be a scalar of its declared type, an operator is not yet known
to suit the field, and no key names anything. That is Task 4's resolve
pass, and a saved view stored without it is a document, not a query.

---

### Task 4: Resolution — keys to ids, values to types, and the dependency list

**Files:**
- Create: `internal/views/resolve.go`
- Create: `internal/views/resolve_test.go`
- Create: `internal/views/testhelpers_test.go`

This is the pass the spec's §4.1 describes: **nothing an agent wrote is
ever concatenated into SQL**, so before a byte of SQL exists, every entity
type key becomes an `entity_types.id`, every relation type key becomes a
`relation_types.id`, every field key becomes its declared `{type,
options}`, and every `{"param": …}` becomes a literal type-checked against
its declaration. After this pass the only agent-controlled values that
reach Postgres are **bind parameters**: uuids, numbers, strings and jsonb
path keys.

**The invariant this task carries:** *isolation is enforced in SQL, not
Go.* The catalogue this pass resolves against is loaded with two
project-filtered statements that already shipped —
`ListEntityTypes(project_id)` and `ListRelationTypes(project_id)` — so a
key that names another game's type resolves to nothing here, before the
compiler can be asked to reach for it. `TestAKeyFromAnotherGameDoesNotResolve`
pins it with a positive control in the same test.

Loading the whole catalogue in two round trips rather than one lookup per
key is deliberate and has a second payoff: **it is the same data
staleness needs** (Task 12), so a run and a staleness report read the game
once between them.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the test helper both this task and every later one uses**

`internal/views/testhelpers_test.go`:

```go
package views

import (
	"context"
	"testing"

	"github.com/google/uuid"
	"github.com/jackc/pgx/v5/pgxpool"

	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/metamodel"
	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/testutil"
)

// game is one seeded game plus the services that write to it. Every
// database test in this package builds one; the fixture is the spec's
// MMORPG worked example, because that is the example the definition of
// done names and a fixture nobody uses end to end is a fixture nobody
// checks.
type game struct {
	pool      *pgxpool.Pool
	meta      *metamodel.Service
	views     *Service
	projectID uuid.UUID
}

// newGame seeds classes, quests and zones with the relations the spec's
// §3.1 example walks: available_to (quest -> class), requires
// (quest -> quest), takes_place_in (quest -> zone).
//
// It seeds **two** games, and returns the first. The second is not
// decoration: every isolation test in this package needs a second game
// with the same keys in it, and a fixture that seeds one game lets a
// missing project filter pass every test in the file.
func newGame(t *testing.T) (*game, *game) {
	t.Helper()
	pool := testutil.NewPool(t)
	build := func(slug string) *game {
		ctx := context.Background()
		var projectID uuid.UUID
		if err := pool.QueryRow(ctx,
			`INSERT INTO projects (slug, name) VALUES ($1, $1) RETURNING id`, slug).
			Scan(&projectID); err != nil {
			t.Fatalf("insert project %s: %v", slug, err)
		}
		meta := metamodel.New(pool, nil)
		g := &game{pool: pool, meta: meta, views: New(pool, nil), projectID: projectID}
		g.seed(t)
		return g
	}
	return build("azeroth"), build("outland")
}

func (g *game) seed(t *testing.T) {
	t.Helper()
	ctx := context.Background()
	minLevel := metamodel.Field{Key: "min_level", Type: metamodel.FieldNumber}
	tags := metamodel.Field{Key: "tags", Type: metamodel.FieldListText}
	rank := metamodel.Field{Key: "rank", Type: metamodel.FieldEnum,
		Options: []string{"common", "rare", "epic"}}

	for _, spec := range []struct {
		key, label, plural string
		schema             metamodel.Schema
	}{
		{"class", "Class", "Classes", nil},
		{"zone", "Zone", "Zones", nil},
		{"quest", "Quest", "Quests", metamodel.Schema{minLevel, tags, rank}},
	} {
		if _, err := g.meta.UpsertEntityType(ctx, g.projectID, metamodel.EntityTypeInput{
			Key: spec.key, Label: spec.label, LabelPlural: spec.plural, Schema: spec.schema,
		}); err != nil {
			t.Fatalf("seed type %s: %v", spec.key, err)
		}
	}
	for _, spec := range []struct{ key, label string }{
		{"available_to", "Available to"}, {"requires", "Requires"},
		{"takes_place_in", "Takes place in"},
	} {
		if _, err := g.meta.UpsertRelationType(ctx, g.projectID, metamodel.RelationTypeInput{
			Key: spec.key, Label: spec.label,
		}); err != nil {
			t.Fatalf("seed relation type %s: %v", spec.key, err)
		}
	}
	g.entity(t, "class", "mage", "Mage", nil)
	g.entity(t, "zone", "elwynn", "Elwynn Forest", nil)
	g.entity(t, "zone", "westfall", "Westfall", nil)
	g.entity(t, "quest", "hogger", "Wanted: Hogger",
		map[string]any{"min_level": 22, "rank": "rare", "tags": []any{"kill", "elite"}})
	g.entity(t, "quest", "defias", "The Defias Brotherhood",
		map[string]any{"min_level": 28, "rank": "epic", "tags": []any{"chain"}})
	g.entity(t, "quest", "cook", "Cooking for Bruises",
		map[string]any{"min_level": 12, "rank": "common", "tags": []any{}})
	g.relate(t, "available_to", "quest", "hogger", "class", "mage")
	g.relate(t, "available_to", "quest", "defias", "class", "mage")
	g.relate(t, "available_to", "quest", "cook", "class", "mage")
	g.relate(t, "requires", "quest", "defias", "quest", "hogger")
	g.relate(t, "takes_place_in", "quest", "hogger", "zone", "elwynn")
	g.relate(t, "takes_place_in", "quest", "defias", "zone", "westfall")
}

func (g *game) entity(t *testing.T, typeKey, key, name string, fields map[string]any) uuid.UUID {
	t.Helper()
	row, err := g.meta.UpsertEntity(context.Background(), g.projectID, metamodel.EntityInput{
		TypeKey: typeKey, Key: key, Name: name, Fields: fields,
	})
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("seed entity %s/%s: %v", typeKey, key, err)
	}
	return row.ID
}

func (g *game) relate(t *testing.T, relKey, srcType, srcKey, tgtType, tgtKey string) {
	t.Helper()
	_, err := g.meta.UpsertRelation(context.Background(), g.projectID, metamodel.RelationInput{
		TypeKey: relKey,
		Source:  metamodel.Ref{TypeKey: srcType, Key: srcKey},
		Target:  metamodel.Ref{TypeKey: tgtType, Key: tgtKey},
	})
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("seed relation %s: %v", relKey, err)
	}
}

// mustParse parses a query that the test asserts is well-formed. A test
// that means to exercise resolution must not be silently exercising
// ParseQuery instead.
func mustParse(t *testing.T, doc string) *Query {
	t.Helper()
	q, err := ParseQuery([]byte(doc))
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("this query must parse; the test means to exercise resolution: %v", err)
	}
	return q
}
```

- [ ] **Step 2: Write the failing resolution tests**

`internal/views/resolve_test.go`:

```go
package views

import (
	"context"
	"errors"
	"strings"
	"testing"

	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/metamodel"
)

func TestAQueryResolvesEveryKeyToAnID(t *testing.T) {
	g, _ := newGame(t)
	q := mustParse(t, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest","as":"q"}],
		"traverse":[{"from":"q","via":"available_to","direction":"out","to_type":"class","as":"c"}]}`)

	r, err := g.views.Resolve(context.Background(), g.projectID, q)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("resolve: %v", err)
	}
	if r.Sets[0].EntityTypeID == nil {
		t.Fatal("the seed selector's type must resolve to an id")
	}
	if len(r.Steps[0].RelationTypeIDs) != 1 {
		t.Fatalf("the step's via must resolve to one relation type id, got %v",
			r.Steps[0].RelationTypeIDs)
	}
}

// TestAKeyFromAnotherGameDoesNotResolve is the isolation test, with its
// positive control in the same test so an empty answer cannot pass it.
func TestAKeyFromAnotherGameDoesNotResolve(t *testing.T) {
	g, other := newGame(t)
	ctx := context.Background()
	// Give the second game a type the first does not have.
	if _, err := other.meta.UpsertEntityType(ctx, other.projectID, metamodel.EntityTypeInput{
		Key: "vehicle", Label: "Vehicle", LabelPlural: "Vehicles",
	}); err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("seed the other game: %v", err)
	}
	// Positive control: it resolves in its own game.
	if _, err := other.views.Resolve(ctx, other.projectID,
		mustParse(t, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"vehicle"}]}`)); err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("vehicle must resolve in the game that declares it: %v", err)
	}
	_, err := g.views.Resolve(ctx, g.projectID,
		mustParse(t, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"vehicle"}]}`))
	if !errors.Is(err, ErrQueryInvalid) {
		t.Fatalf("a type from another game must not resolve, got %v", err)
	}
	if !strings.Contains(err.Error(), `no entity type "vehicle" in this game`) {
		t.Fatalf("must name the key back, got %v", err)
	}
}

func TestAnOperatorTheFieldTypeCannotAnswerIsRefusedAtSaveTime(t *testing.T) {
	g, _ := newGame(t)
	_, err := g.views.Resolve(context.Background(), g.projectID,
		mustParse(t, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest","where":{"field":"rank","op":"gt","value":"rare"}}]}`))
	if !errors.Is(err, ErrQueryInvalid) {
		t.Fatalf("gt on an enum must be refused, got %v", err)
	}
	if !strings.Contains(err.Error(),
		`the field "rank" is declared enum, which answers eq, neq, in, exists`) {
		t.Fatalf("must name the type and the operators it does answer, got %v", err)
	}
}

// TestAnEnumValueOutsideItsOptionsIsRefusedRatherThanEmpty is the failure
// mode the spec calls the most expensive one in a query language whose
// author is not in the room.
func TestAnEnumValueOutsideItsOptionsIsRefusedRatherThanEmpty(t *testing.T) {
	g, _ := newGame(t)
	_, err := g.views.Resolve(context.Background(), g.projectID,
		mustParse(t, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest","where":{"field":"rank","op":"eq","value":"legendary"}}]}`))
	if !errors.Is(err, ErrQueryInvalid) {
		t.Fatalf("an option outside the declaration must be refused, got %v", err)
	}
	if !strings.Contains(err.Error(), `"legendary" is not one of [common rare epic]`) {
		t.Fatalf("must list the options, got %v", err)
	}
}

func TestAParameterIsTypeCheckedAgainstTheOperatorItFeeds(t *testing.T) {
	g, _ := newGame(t)
	ctx := context.Background()
	q := mustParse(t, `{"v":1,"params":[{"key":"floor","type":"number","default":20}],
		"from":[{"type":"quest","where":{"field":"min_level","op":"gte","value":{"param":"floor"}}}]}`)
	if _, err := g.views.Resolve(ctx, g.projectID, q); err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("a number param feeding gte on a number field must resolve: %v", err)
	}

	bad := mustParse(t, `{"v":1,"params":[{"key":"who","type":"text","default":"mage"}],
		"from":[{"type":"quest","where":{"field":"min_level","op":"gte","value":{"param":"who"}}}]}`)
	_, err := g.views.Resolve(ctx, g.projectID, bad)
	if !errors.Is(err, ErrQueryInvalid) {
		t.Fatalf("a text param feeding a number field must be refused, got %v", err)
	}
	if !strings.Contains(err.Error(), `the parameter "who" is declared text and the field `+
		`"min_level" is declared number`) {
		t.Fatalf("must name both sides, got %v", err)
	}

	unbound := mustParse(t, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest",
		"where":{"field":"min_level","op":"gte","value":{"param":"nowhere"}}}]}`)
	_, err = g.views.Resolve(ctx, g.projectID, unbound)
	if !strings.Contains(err.Error(), `no parameter named "nowhere" is declared`) {
		t.Fatalf("an unbound param must be named back, got %v", err)
	}
}

// TestResolutionListsEveryTypeReferenceWithItsPointer is what Task 11
// writes into view_refs and what Task 12's staleness report reads back.
func TestResolutionListsEveryTypeReferenceWithItsPointer(t *testing.T) {
	g, _ := newGame(t)
	q := mustParse(t, `{"v":1,"from":[{"type":"quest","as":"q"}],
		"traverse":[{"from":"q","via":["available_to","requires"],"to_type":"class","as":"c"}],
		"project":{"color_by":{"related":{"via":"takes_place_in","type":"zone","attr":"@name"}}}}`)
	r, err := g.views.Resolve(context.Background(), g.projectID, q)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("resolve: %v", err)
	}
	want := map[string]string{
		"/from/0/type":            "quest",
		"/traverse/0/via/0":       "available_to",
		"/traverse/0/via/1":       "requires",
		"/traverse/0/to_type/0":   "class",
		"/project/color_by/related/via":  "takes_place_in",
		"/project/color_by/related/type": "zone",
	}
	got := map[string]string{}
	for _, ref := range r.Refs {
		got[ref.Pointer] = ref.Key
		if ref.ID == nil {
			t.Errorf("%s resolved to no id, and every key here exists", ref.Pointer)
		}
	}
	if len(got) != len(want) {
		t.Fatalf("listed %d references, want %d: %v", len(got), len(want), got)
	}
	for ptr, key := range want {
		if got[ptr] != key {
			t.Errorf("%s: got %q want %q", ptr, got[ptr], key)
		}
	}
}
```

- [ ] **Step 4: Write `internal/views/service.go`**

```go
package views

import (
	"context"
	"fmt"

	"github.com/google/uuid"
	"github.com/jackc/pgx/v5"
	"github.com/jackc/pgx/v5/pgxpool"

	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/db/dbq"
	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/metamodel"
	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/realtime"
	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/roles"
)

// Actor is the metamodel's, aliased rather than redeclared: the audit
// columns on views are the same two columns, filled from the same caller.
type Actor = metamodel.Actor

// Service is the views domain.
type Service struct {
	pool *pgxpool.Pool
	q    *dbq.Queries
	hub  *realtime.Hub
}

// New builds the service. The hub may be nil, in which case nothing is
// published; the package's own tests run that way.
func New(pool *pgxpool.Pool, hub *realtime.Hub) *Service {
	return &Service{pool: pool, q: dbq.New(pool), hub: hub}
}

// publish emits a change event, if a hub is attached. minRole and
// humanOnly are passed explicitly rather than inferred from kind, so each
// call site shows the gating it chose; events.go argues each pair.
//
// **Every caller must call this after withTx has returned, never from
// inside fn.** An event published inside the transaction announces a
// change that may still roll back, and a subscriber that re-reads on
// hearing it would read the state before the change and cache it as the
// state after. Task 11's TestNoViewEventIsPublishedWhenTheCommitFails
// pins the one placement a refusal test cannot catch — a publish as the
// last statement inside fn — with a deferred constraint that makes the
// commit, and only the commit, fail.
func (s *Service) publish(projectID uuid.UUID, kind string, minRole roles.Role, humanOnly bool, payload any) {
	if s.hub == nil {
		return
	}
	s.hub.Publish(realtime.Event{
		ProjectID: projectID, Kind: kind,
		MinRole: string(minRole), HumanOnly: humanOnly, Payload: payload,
	})
}

// withTx runs fn inside a transaction, rolling back unless it returns nil.
func (s *Service) withTx(ctx context.Context, fn func(*dbq.Queries) error) error {
	tx, err := s.pool.Begin(ctx)
	if err != nil {
		return fmt.Errorf("begin: %w", err)
	}
	defer func() { _ = tx.Rollback(ctx) }()
	if err := fn(dbq.New(tx)); err != nil {
		return err
	}
	if err := tx.Commit(ctx); err != nil {
		return fmt.Errorf("commit: %w", err)
	}
	return nil
}

// notFound maps pgx's no-rows sentinel onto a named miss.
func notFound(err error, missing error, doing string) error {
	if err == nil {
		return nil
	}
	if errorsIsNoRows(err) {
		return missing
	}
	return fmt.Errorf("%s: %w", doing, err)
}

func errorsIsNoRows(err error) bool { return errors.Is(err, pgx.ErrNoRows) }
```

(add `"errors"` to the imports.)

- [ ] **Step 5: Write `internal/views/resolve.go`**

```go
package views

import (
	"context"
	"fmt"
	"sort"
	"strings"

	"github.com/google/uuid"

	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/db/dbq"
	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/metamodel"
)

// Catalogue is one game's declared vocabulary, read once and reused by
// resolution, by compilation and by the staleness report.
//
// It is loaded with two project-filtered statements that already shipped,
// ListEntityTypes and ListRelationTypes, rather than one lookup per key.
// Two reasons: a query with eight steps would otherwise make a dozen
// round trips inside a call that is already the most expensive one in the
// product, and staleness (Task 12) needs exactly this data, so a run and
// its staleness report read the game once between them.
//
// Keys are folded to lower case in the maps because the database folds
// them: entity_types_key_key is UNIQUE (project_id, lower(key)). The
// *stored* spelling is kept on the row, which is what a rename diagnostic
// compares against.
type Catalogue struct {
	EntityTypes   map[string]dbq.EntityType
	RelationTypes map[string]dbq.RelationType
	schemas       map[uuid.UUID]metamodel.Schema
}

// LoadCatalogue reads one game's vocabulary.
func (s *Service) LoadCatalogue(ctx context.Context, projectID uuid.UUID) (*Catalogue, error) {
	types, err := s.q.ListEntityTypes(ctx, projectID)
	if err != nil {
		return nil, fmt.Errorf("list entity types: %w", err)
	}
	relTypes, err := s.q.ListRelationTypes(ctx, projectID)
	if err != nil {
		return nil, fmt.Errorf("list relation types: %w", err)
	}
	cat := &Catalogue{
		EntityTypes:   make(map[string]dbq.EntityType, len(types)),
		RelationTypes: make(map[string]dbq.RelationType, len(relTypes)),
		schemas:       make(map[uuid.UUID]metamodel.Schema, len(types)+len(relTypes)),
	}
	for _, row := range types {
		cat.EntityTypes[strings.ToLower(row.Key)] = row
		schema, err := metamodel.ParseSchema(row.FieldSchema)
		if err != nil {
			return nil, fmt.Errorf("parse field schema of entity type %q: %w", row.Key, err)
		}
		cat.schemas[row.ID] = schema
	}
	for _, row := range relTypes {
		cat.RelationTypes[strings.ToLower(row.Key)] = row
		schema, err := metamodel.ParseSchema(row.FieldSchema)
		if err != nil {
			return nil, fmt.Errorf("parse field schema of relation type %q: %w", row.Key, err)
		}
		cat.schemas[row.ID] = schema
	}
	return cat, nil
}

// TypeRef is one reference from a query to a declared type, with the
// pointer that addresses it. It is what Task 11 writes into view_refs and
// what Task 12's staleness report reads back.
type TypeRef struct {
	Kind    string // "entity_type" or "relation_type"
	Key     string // as the query spells it
	ID      *uuid.UUID
	Pointer string
}

// ResolvedSet is one seed selector or one step's output, with its keys
// turned into ids.
type ResolvedSet struct {
	Name         string
	EntityTypeID *uuid.UUID
	EntityIDs    []uuid.UUID
	Where        *ResolvedPredicate
	Selector     *Selector
}

// ResolvedStep is one traversal with its relation types resolved.
type ResolvedStep struct {
	Step            *Step
	Name            string
	FromSet         string
	RelationTypeIDs []uuid.UUID
	ToTypeIDs       []uuid.UUID
	Where           *ResolvedPredicate
	EdgeWhere       *ResolvedPredicate
}

// ResolvedPredicate is a predicate whose every leaf carries the declared
// type of the field it names and a value already coerced to that type.
type ResolvedPredicate struct {
	All  []ResolvedPredicate
	Any  []ResolvedPredicate
	Not  *ResolvedPredicate
	Leaf *ResolvedLeaf
}

// ResolvedLeaf is one comparison, ready to compile. Value is a Go value
// the compiler binds as a parameter and never spells into SQL.
type ResolvedLeaf struct {
	Field    FieldRef
	Type     metamodel.FieldType
	Op       Operator
	Value    any
	Values   []any
	Pointer  string
}

// Resolved is a query with every name turned into something the compiler
// can bind.
type Resolved struct {
	Query  *Query
	Cat    *Catalogue
	Sets   []ResolvedSet
	Steps  []ResolvedStep
	Refs   []TypeRef
	Params map[string]any
	Limits ResolvedLimits
}

// ResolvedLimits are the bounds this run will use: the query's overrides
// where it gave them, the defaults otherwise, both already known to be
// within the hard caps because ParseQuery refused anything else.
type ResolvedLimits struct {
	MaxDepth int
	MaxNodes int
	MaxEdges int
}

// Resolve turns a parsed query into one the compiler can emit SQL for,
// against one game.
//
// **Nothing it produces is ever concatenated into SQL.** Every key
// becomes a uuid, every field key becomes a declared type plus the jsonb
// path key the compiler binds as a parameter, and every value becomes a
// Go value. After this pass the compiler has nothing left that a caller
// controls except bind parameters — which is the property that makes the
// injection question answerable rather than a matter of care.
//
// It collects every problem in one pass. An agent writing a five-step
// traversal against an unfamiliar game gets all five mistakes at once.
func (s *Service) Resolve(ctx context.Context, projectID uuid.UUID, q *Query) (*Resolved, error) {
	cat, err := s.LoadCatalogue(ctx, projectID)
	if err != nil {
		return nil, err
	}
	return ResolveAgainst(cat, q)
}

// ResolveAgainst is Resolve with the catalogue already in hand, for a
// caller that has just read it — Task 12's staleness report, and Task 7's
// execution, which resolves and compiles in one pass over one read.
func ResolveAgainst(cat *Catalogue, q *Query) (*Resolved, error) {
	r := &Resolved{Query: q, Cat: cat, Params: map[string]any{}}
	var problems []metamodel.FieldError
	add := func(ptr, message string) {
		problems = append(problems, metamodel.FieldError{Path: ptr, Message: message})
	}

	// Parameters first: a predicate that feeds one has to know its type.
	paramTypes := map[string]metamodel.FieldType{}
	for i, p := range q.Params {
		paramTypes[p.Key] = metamodel.FieldType(p.Type)
		if p.Default == nil {
			continue
		}
		value, err := coerceParam(metamodel.FieldType(p.Type), p.Default)
		if err != nil {
			add(pointer("params", i, "default"), err.Error())
			continue
		}
		r.Params[p.Key] = value
	}

	entityType := func(ptr, key string) *dbq.EntityType {
		row, ok := cat.EntityTypes[strings.ToLower(key)]
		if !ok {
			add(ptr, fmt.Sprintf("no entity type %q in this game: declare it with types.upsert, "+
				"or use types.list to see what this game has", key))
			r.Refs = append(r.Refs, TypeRef{Kind: "entity_type", Key: key, Pointer: ptr})
			return nil
		}
		id := row.ID
		r.Refs = append(r.Refs, TypeRef{Kind: "entity_type", Key: key, ID: &id, Pointer: ptr})
		return &row
	}
	relationType := func(ptr, key string) *dbq.RelationType {
		row, ok := cat.RelationTypes[strings.ToLower(key)]
		if !ok {
			add(ptr, fmt.Sprintf("no relation type %q in this game: declare it with "+
				"relation_types.upsert, or use relation_types.list to see what this game has", key))
			r.Refs = append(r.Refs, TypeRef{Kind: "relation_type", Key: key, Pointer: ptr})
			return nil
		}
		id := row.ID
		r.Refs = append(r.Refs, TypeRef{Kind: "relation_type", Key: key, ID: &id, Pointer: ptr})
		return &row
	}

	for i := range q.From {
		sel := &q.From[i]
		ptr := pointer("from", i)
		set := ResolvedSet{Name: sel.As, Selector: sel}
		if row := entityType(ptr+"/type", sel.Type); row != nil {
			id := row.ID
			set.EntityTypeID = &id
			set.Where = resolvePredicate(cat.schemas[row.ID], paramTypes, sel.Where,
				ptr+"/where", &problems)
		}
		r.Sets = append(r.Sets, set)
	}

	for i := range q.Traverse {
		step := &q.Traverse[i]
		ptr := pointer("traverse", i)
		rs := ResolvedStep{Step: step, Name: step.As, FromSet: step.From}
		var edgeSchema metamodel.Schema
		for j, key := range step.Via {
			if row := relationType(pointer("traverse", i, "via", j), key); row != nil {
				rs.RelationTypeIDs = append(rs.RelationTypeIDs, row.ID)
				if j == 0 {
					edgeSchema = cat.schemas[row.ID]
				}
			}
		}
		var nodeSchema metamodel.Schema
		for j, key := range step.ToType {
			if row := entityType(pointer("traverse", i, "to_type", j), key); row != nil {
				rs.ToTypeIDs = append(rs.ToTypeIDs, row.ID)
				if j == 0 {
					nodeSchema = cat.schemas[row.ID]
				}
			}
		}
		// A step with no to_type reaches entities of any type, so a
		// predicate on it can only name built-ins. resolvePredicate is
		// told so by an empty schema, and says exactly that when a
		// declared field key appears.
		rs.Where = resolvePredicate(nodeSchema, paramTypes, step.Where, ptr+"/where", &problems)
		rs.EdgeWhere = resolvePredicate(edgeSchema, paramTypes, step.EdgeWhere,
			ptr+"/edge_where", &problems)
		r.Steps = append(r.Steps, rs)
	}

	// The projection's one-hop related attributes are type references too,
	// and a stale one breaks a colour rather than a filter — which is the
	// silent failure the whole view_refs table exists to make loud.
	for name, ref := range map[string]*AttrRef{
		"label": q.Project.Label, "color_by": q.Project.ColorBy,
		"group_by": q.Project.GroupBy, "size_by": q.Project.SizeBy,
		"sort_by": q.Project.SortBy,
	} {
		if ref == nil || ref.Related == nil {
			continue
		}
		relationType(pointer("project", name, "related", "via"), ref.Related.Via)
		if ref.Related.Type != "" {
			entityType(pointer("project", name, "related", "type"), ref.Related.Type)
		}
	}

	r.Limits = ResolvedLimits{
		MaxDepth: DefaultMaxDepth, MaxNodes: DefaultMaxNodes, MaxEdges: DefaultMaxEdges,
	}
	if l := q.Limits; l != nil {
		if l.MaxDepth != nil {
			r.Limits.MaxDepth = *l.MaxDepth
		}
		if l.MaxNodes != nil {
			r.Limits.MaxNodes = *l.MaxNodes
		}
		if l.MaxEdges != nil {
			r.Limits.MaxEdges = *l.MaxEdges
		}
	}
	for i, step := range q.Traverse {
		if step.Depth != nil && step.Depth.Max > r.Limits.MaxDepth {
			problems = append(problems, metamodel.FieldError{
				Path: pointer("traverse", i, "depth"),
				Message: fmt.Sprintf("asks for depth %d and this query's max_depth is %d: "+
					"raise limits.max_depth (up to %d) or lower this step",
					step.Depth.Max, r.Limits.MaxDepth, HardMaxDepth),
			})
		}
	}

	if len(problems) > 0 {
		sort.SliceStable(problems, func(i, j int) bool { return problems[i].Path < problems[j].Path })
		return nil, invalidQueryProblems(problems)
	}
	return r, nil
}

// resolvePredicate walks a predicate tree, giving every leaf the declared
// type of the field it names and coercing its value to that type.
func resolvePredicate(schema metamodel.Schema, paramTypes map[string]metamodel.FieldType,
	p *Predicate, ptr string, problems *[]metamodel.FieldError) *ResolvedPredicate {
	if p == nil {
		return nil
	}
	add := func(at, message string) {
		*problems = append(*problems, metamodel.FieldError{Path: at, Message: message})
	}
	switch {
	case p.All != nil:
		out := &ResolvedPredicate{}
		for i := range p.All {
			if child := resolvePredicate(schema, paramTypes, &p.All[i],
				ptr+"/all"+pointer(i), problems); child != nil {
				out.All = append(out.All, *child)
			}
		}
		return out
	case p.Any != nil:
		out := &ResolvedPredicate{}
		for i := range p.Any {
			if child := resolvePredicate(schema, paramTypes, &p.Any[i],
				ptr+"/any"+pointer(i), problems); child != nil {
				out.Any = append(out.Any, *child)
			}
		}
		return out
	case p.Not != nil:
		return &ResolvedPredicate{Not: resolvePredicate(schema, paramTypes, p.Not,
			ptr+"/not", problems)}
	}

	leaf := &ResolvedLeaf{Field: p.FieldRef, Op: Operator(p.Op), Pointer: ptr}
	var declared metamodel.Field
	switch {
	case p.FieldRef.Builtin:
		leaf.Type = builtinTypes[p.FieldRef.Key]
	default:
		found := false
		for _, f := range schema {
			if f.Key == p.FieldRef.Key {
				declared, found = f, true
				break
			}
		}
		if !found {
			add(ptr+"/field", fmt.Sprintf(
				"no field %q is declared on this type: use types.get to see its field_schema, "+
					"or address a built-in with an @ sigil", p.FieldRef.Key))
			return nil
		}
		leaf.Type = declared.Type
	}

	if !AdmitsOperator(leaf.Type, leaf.Op) {
		names := make([]string, 0, len(OperatorsFor(leaf.Type)))
		for _, op := range OperatorsFor(leaf.Type) {
			names = append(names, string(op))
		}
		add(ptr+"/op", fmt.Sprintf("the field %q is declared %s, which answers %s — not %q",
			p.FieldRef.Key, leaf.Type, strings.Join(names, ", "), p.Op))
		return nil
	}

	// A parameter in a value position: its declared type has to match the
	// field's, and the mismatch names both sides because an agent holding
	// only one of them cannot tell which to change.
	if ref, ok := paramRefOf(p.Value); ok {
		paramType, declaredParam := paramTypes[ref.Key]
		switch {
		case !declaredParam:
			add(ptr+"/value", fmt.Sprintf("no parameter named %q is declared: add it to "+
				"params, or write the value out", ref.Key))
		case paramType != leaf.Type:
			add(ptr+"/value", fmt.Sprintf("the parameter %q is declared %s and the field %q "+
				"is declared %s", ref.Key, paramType, p.FieldRef.Key, leaf.Type))
		default:
			leaf.Value = ParamRef{Key: ref.Key}
		}
		return &ResolvedPredicate{Leaf: leaf}
	}

	switch shapeOf[leaf.Op] {
	case shapeList, shapePair:
		for i, raw := range p.Value.([]any) {
			value, err := coerceOperand(leaf.Type, declared, raw)
			if err != nil {
				add(ptr+pointer("value", i), err.Error())
				continue
			}
			leaf.Values = append(leaf.Values, value)
		}
	case shapeBool, shapeNumber:
		leaf.Value = p.Value
	default:
		value, err := coerceOperand(leaf.Type, declared, p.Value)
		if err != nil {
			add(ptr+"/value", err.Error())
			return nil
		}
		leaf.Value = value
	}
	return &ResolvedPredicate{Leaf: leaf}
}

// coerceOperand checks one literal against the declared type it will be
// compared with, and is where an enum option outside its declaration is
// refused rather than becoming an empty result.
//
// It reuses metamodel.Schema.Validate rather than reimplementing the
// coercion: a single-field schema whose one field is the declared one,
// validated against a single-value map, gives exactly the metamodel's own
// judgement and exactly its wording. A second copy of that judgement is
// how a query starts accepting values the write path refuses.
func coerceOperand(typ metamodel.FieldType, declared metamodel.Field, raw any) (any, error) {
	if typ == TypeTimestamp {
		s, ok := raw.(string)
		if !ok {
			return nil, fmt.Errorf("expected an RFC 3339 timestamp, got %T", raw)
		}
		at, err := time.Parse(time.RFC3339, s)
		if err != nil {
			return nil, fmt.Errorf("must be an RFC 3339 timestamp such as "+
				"2026-09-02T10:00:00Z (got %q)", s)
		}
		return at, nil
	}
	field := declared
	field.Key, field.Type, field.Required, field.HasDefault = "value", typ, false, false
	// list<text> is compared element-wise by every operator it admits, so
	// an operand is one element and is judged as text.
	if typ == metamodel.FieldListText {
		field.Type = metamodel.FieldText
	}
	out, err := metamodel.Schema{field}.Validate(map[string]any{"value": raw})
	if err != nil {
		var ve *metamodel.ValidationError
		if errors.As(err, &ve) && len(ve.Fields) > 0 {
			return nil, errors.New(ve.Fields[0].Message)
		}
		return nil, err
	}
	return out["value"], nil
}

// coerceParam checks a parameter's default, and a value bound at run
// time, against the parameter's own declared type.
func coerceParam(typ metamodel.FieldType, raw any) (any, error) {
	return coerceOperand(typ, metamodel.Field{Type: typ}, raw)
}
```

Add `"errors"` and `"time"` to the import block.

**Corrections from implementing Task 4** (landed as `fc19f4d`; every
mutation below was applied to the shipped code and watched fail the test
that claims it, then restored, with the baseline confirmed green in
between). Eight findings. Four of them change a code block a later task
depends on.

1. **The catalogue reads through `internal/metamodel`'s accessors, not
   through `dbq` directly.** This task's block called
   `s.q.ListEntityTypes(ctx, projectID)` off a `dbq.Queries` of its own,
   which would have made `internal/views` the fourth place in this
   repository that spells the isolation rule. `LoadCatalogue` calls
   `metamodel.Service.ListEntityTypes` and `ListRelationTypes` — both
   already take a project id and already filter on it in SQL — so this
   package has no project-filtered statement of its own to get wrong.
   `Service` therefore holds a `*metamodel.Service` rather than a
   `*dbq.Queries`, and Task 11 adds its own `dbq` handle for the saved-
   view CRUD beside it.

2. **`service.go` ships only what Task 4 uses.** The block's `publish`,
   `withTx`, `notFound` and `errorsIsNoRows` are all unreferenced until
   Task 11, and `golangci-lint`'s `unused` linter is enabled in this
   repository, so committing them here fails `make lint`. They move to
   Task 11, unchanged, along with the `realtime` and `dbq` imports they
   need. The `publish` doc comment's placement rule — after `withTx`,
   never inside `fn` — moves with them.

3. **`ResolvedSet.EntityIDs` was declared and could never be filled.**
   Task 6's `compileSelector` compiles a selector's `keys` shortcut to
   `lower(e.key) = ANY($m)` over the keys themselves, bound as a text
   array, so there is no entity lookup for this pass to make and nothing
   to put in the field. It is removed rather than left as a nil slice a
   later task might read as "this selector named no keys": the same shape
   as a comment claiming more than the code does. `Selector` stays on
   `ResolvedSet`, which is where the compiler reads the keys from.

4. **A predicate on a step was judged against one schema when a step
   reaches several types.** The block took `cat.schemas[row.ID]` of the
   *first* `to_type` and the *first* `via`, which is wrong in both
   directions: a field declared on one of two reached types compiles to a
   comparison that silently matches nothing on the other, and a key
   declared `number` on one type and `enum` on another is coerced against
   whichever the document happened to list first. What ships is a
   `fieldScope` holding every schema in scope, admitting a field only when
   every type declares it with the same type — and the same options, for
   an enum — and naming the type that lacks it or the two declarations
   that disagree.
   `TestAFieldMustBeDeclaredTheSameWayOnEveryTypeAStepReaches` pins it,
   with the single-type case as its positive control.

5. **The open-step message blamed a type the caller never named.** The
   block's comment said a step with no `to_type` "says exactly that when
   a declared field key appears", and the code it described answered `no
   field "min_level" is declared on this type` — over an empty schema
   belonging to no type. `fieldScope.open` makes the refusal say that the
   step reaches entities of any type, that no field schema applies, and
   what the two ways out are (name a `to_type`, or compare a built-in),
   listing the built-in vocabulary. `TestAStepWithoutAToTypeAdmitsOnlyBuiltins`
   pins the wording and carries a built-in in the same position as its
   positive control — the claim was previously in a comment and in no
   assertion, which is this project's first standing defect exactly.

6. **`coerceOperand` took the metamodel's whole judgement, including the
   declared range, and that refuses legal queries.** `Min` and `Max` are
   rules about what may be *written*. A stored value can sit outside them
   — the metamodel flags an entity invalid on a schema change and leaves
   its values in place, which is what `include_invalid` exists for — so
   `difficulty gte 0` against a field declared `min: 1` is an ordinary
   "everything" query and not a mistake. The bounds are cleared before
   the single-field schema is validated; the enum's `options` are kept,
   and the asymmetry is the point: a value outside an enum's options is a
   misspelling the refusal can name, while a number outside a range is a
   legitimate question.
   `TestADeclaredRangeDoesNotRefuseAComparisonOutsideIt` pins both halves,
   its positive control being that text against a number field is still
   refused in the metamodel's own wording. **The fixture moved with this**:
   `quest` gains a `difficulty` field declaring `min: 1, max: 10`, because
   a fixture where no field declares a range cannot tell the two rules
   apart.

7. **The projection's five attribute positions are read off
   `projectionAttrs`, not listed again.** The block wrote them as a
   `map[string]*AttrRef` literal, which is a fourth enumerate-don't-walk
   position — and a non-deterministic one, since ranging a map would have
   made `Refs` order vary per run. Reading Task 3's ordered table means
   `TestEveryProjectionAttributeReferenceHasALineInTheTable` covers this
   pass too: a sixth `*AttrRef` on `Projection` fails that test, and the
   line it then gains is the line this loop reads. The block also
   dereferenced `q.Project` unguarded; `applyDefaults` never leaves it
   nil, but `ResolveAgainst` is exported and takes a `*Query` a Go caller
   may have built by hand, so it is nil-guarded.

8. **A refused query still has a dependency list, and Task 12 needs it.**
   `ResolveAgainst` answers an unresolvable key with an error and no
   `*Resolved`, so there was nothing for a staleness report — whose whole
   subject is a query that no longer resolves — to read `Refs` off. The
   pass is split: an unexported `resolveInto` always returns what it
   built alongside its problems, `ResolveAgainst` turns the problems into
   the refusal, and `ReferencesOf(cat, q)` returns the list alone. One
   implementation, so the list a stale view reports cannot drift from the
   list a saved view stored.
   `TestAReferenceThatDoesNotResolveIsStillListedWithItsKey` pins that a
   dead reference keeps its kind, its key and its pointer with a nil id,
   with a resolving reference in the same assertion as its control.

**Two things reached from the shipped code rather than assumed:**

- Two refusals in `resolvePredicate` are unreachable from `ParseQuery`
  and ship anyway, each with a comment saying so: an unknown `@` sigil
  (`checkPredicate` refuses it first) and a non-list value under a list
  operator (`checkValueShape` refuses it first). `ResolveAgainst` is
  exported and a hand-built `Query` has been through neither pass, and a
  panic on a type assertion is not a refusal a caller can act on. Neither
  comment claims a guarantee; both say what they are reachable from.
- Eight tests beyond the block's six, each closing something the block
  stated and nothing held:
  `TestARelationTypeFromAnotherGameDoesNotResolve` (the isolation
  assertion one statement along — a project filter missing from the
  relation-type read alone passes every other test in the file),
  `TestAParameterDefaultIsCoercedToItsDeclaredType`,
  `TestAStepWithoutAToTypeAdmitsOnlyBuiltins`,
  `TestAFieldMustBeDeclaredTheSameWayOnEveryTypeAStepReaches`,
  `TestADeclaredRangeDoesNotRefuseAComparisonOutsideIt`,
  `TestAReferenceThatDoesNotResolveIsStillListedWithItsKey`,
  `TestEveryProblemInOneQueryIsReportedInOnePass` (the one-pass promise
  the doc comment makes, asserted as a count and an order),
  `TestAStepDeeperThanTheQueryAllowsIsRefused` and
  `TestResolutionTakesTheQuerysLimitsAndDefaultsTheRest` — the latter two
  being the first readers of Task 3's `Default*` constants, which shipped
  declared and unread.

**Recorded for Tasks 6 and 12**, verified rather than assumed:

- **`@type` resolves as text, not as a type id.** A leaf `{"field":
  "@type", "op": "eq", "value": "quest"}` carries the string `"quest"`
  out of this pass; Task 6's block says the built-in compiles against the
  resolved type id, and `r.Cat.EntityTypes` is what it looks that up in.
  Two consequences Task 6 owns rather than inherits: a misspelled type
  name in that position is an *empty result* today rather than a
  refusal, and it contributes **no `TypeRef`**, so renaming a type does
  not make a view naming it in `@type` report stale. Both are decidable
  either way; neither is decided here, because adding the reference would
  change what `view_refs` means (a value, not a structural dependency)
  and that is Task 11 and Task 12's table.
- **A run-time parameter binding has no pass yet.** `Resolved.Params`
  holds the declared *defaults*, coerced. `coerceParam` is the function a
  caller-supplied binding goes through, and Task 15's `views.run` is what
  calls it; nothing in this task binds a value at run time, and
  `Resolved.Params` must not be read as "the values this run will use"
  until it does.
- **`Catalogue.schemas` is unexported and keyed by type id.** Task 12
  needs the *stored spelling* of a key for a rename diagnostic, which is

**Corrections from reviewing Task 4** (landed as `3954c3d`, on top of
`fc19f4d` and `1d9bec0`). Every mutation named below was applied to the
shipped code, watched fail the test that claims it, and restored, with
the baseline confirmed green — and confirmed *running*, `-v`, since this
package's tests skip silently without `TEST_DATABASE_URL`.

9. **The catalogue had no identity, and that was the one route this
   package's isolation story did not defend.** Loading one game's
   catalogue and calling `ResolveAgainst` with a query meant for another
   resolved, handing back type ids belonging to the game the catalogue
   was read for. The damage was bounded only because Task 6's compiler
   binds the project separately, so such a run returns nothing — but
   `Resolved.Cat` is where Tasks 6, 7 and 12 read type ids and stored
   spellings, and **Task 12 would have compared a saved view against the
   wrong game's vocabulary and answered "not stale"**. The whole isolation
   argument rested on callers pairing two arguments correctly, which is
   the one thing reading through a single project-scoped path was meant
   to remove. `Catalogue` now carries `ProjectID`, `LoadCatalogue` fills
   it, and both entry points that take a catalogue take the game as well
   and refuse a mismatch with `ErrWrongGame` — a programming error, not a
   `*QueryError`, since no document reaches it. **Two signatures changed
   for Tasks 6, 7, 11 and 12**: `ResolveAgainst(projectID, cat, q)`, and
   `ReferencesOf(projectID, cat, q) ([]TypeRef, error)`.
   `TestACatalogueFromAnotherGameIsRefused` is the proof: both games
   declare `quest`, so the query resolves against either catalogue and
   only their identities differ; it asserts the refusal on both entry
   points and carries the same-game call as its control. Mutation:
   dropping the `belongsTo` call is red.

10. **Both mismatch messages named the wrong type.** `fieldScope.field`
    paired the *first* declaring type's name with the declaration of the
    type currently being examined, so a field declared `number` on
    `quest` and `enum` on `region` was reported as *declared number on
    "region" and enum elsewhere* — and a designer following that message
    edits the type that is not the one being described. Reversing the
    `to_type` list reversed the lie symmetrically, and the enum-options
    branch printed one type's options against the other type's name. The
    first declaring type's key is now kept in `foundOn` and each type is
    named beside its own declaration, in both branches.
    `TestAFieldDeclaredTwoWaysNamesEachTypeWithItsOwnDeclaration` pins
    both branches in both orders. It also closes the gap correction 4 and
    `fieldScope`'s doc comment both papered over: the test named there
    pins only the not-declared-everywhere direction, and neither branch
    of the disagreement check was held — replacing the type comparison
    with a constant false, and making the options comparison always true,
    each left the package green. Both mutations are now red, as is
    swapping the two names in the message.
    **The fixture moved with this**: `region` declares `min_level` as an
    `enum` where `quest` declares it a `number`, and `faction` declares
    `rank` with a genuinely different option set.

11. **`sameOptions` compared enum options in order** — the eighth
    instance of the defect class correction 6 fixed one function earlier,
    a rule that refuses a query with no ambiguity in it. Two types
    declaring the same three options in a different order admit exactly
    the same values, and the refusal's own justification, *a value legal
    for one is not legal for the other*, is false in precisely that case.
    Options are now compared as a **set**. The identical-`Type`
    requirement stays, and `min`, `max` and `required` are still allowed
    to diverge: what a comparison needs is that the same value means the
    same thing on every type in scope, which identical type plus
    identical option set gives.
    `TestEnumOptionsAreComparedAsASetNotASequence` pins it, its control
    being that a genuinely different set is still refused. Mutations:
    always-true, and order-sensitive again, are both red.

12. **The third arm of the value switch stored the caller's value
    unchecked** while its comment said the shape had already been judged.
    Its two neighbours guard for a reason that applies to it word for
    word — `ResolveAgainst` is exported and a hand-built `Query` has been
    through no parse pass — and without a guard an `exists` with a string
    and a `length_gte` with a map both resolved and landed where Task 6
    binds them. `exists`/`empty` now require a `bool` and the length
    operators a `float64`, in `checkValueShape`'s own wording.
    `TestAHandBuiltOperandOfTheWrongShapeIsRefused` pins both, with the
    well-shaped operands as controls; each dropped guard is red.

13. **Only half the dead-reference promise was held.** Deleting the
    reference append in the entity-type miss branch survived the suite,
    because the test used a relation type only — and "which entity type
    died" is precisely what Task 12 reports.
    `TestAReferenceThatDoesNotResolveIsStillListedWithItsKey` now kills
    one reference of each kind in one query; both deletions are red.

14. **Case folding was pinned on neither side.** The behaviour was right
    and `Catalogue`'s comment about the folding index was accurate;
    nothing held it, and folding one side only refuses a spelling the
    write path accepted. `TestACatalogueFoldsCaseOnBothSides` asks for
    `QUEST`/`Available_To`/`Class` and checks the reference keeps the
    query's own spelling. The load side needs a *stored* spelling that is
    not already folded, or a catalogue that folds neither side passes:
    the fixture gains the entity type `Boss` and the relation type
    `Guards`, asked for in lower case. All four mutations — folding
    removed from either map, on either side — are red.

15. **Problems are reported in document order, and the sort is gone.**
    Sorting by pointer string puts `/from/10` before `/from/2`.
    `resolveInto` already walks in document order, so the fix is to
    remove the sort rather than to improve it — which required settling
    the limits *before* the traverse loop and judging each step's depth
    inside it, since that check ran last and reported `/traverse/N/depth`
    after `/project/...`. `TestProblemsAreReportedInDocumentOrderNotPointerOrder`
    pins it with problems at `/from/2` and `/from/10`.
    **`ParseQuery` had the same defect and is fixed differently, on
    purpose**: `checkQuery` reports the whole-document text bounds before
    the per-member checks, so its problems never arrive in document order
    and removing its sort would make the order worse, not better. It
    sorts with a new `pointerLess`, which compares pointers segment by
    segment and index segments numerically.
    `TestASetOfProblemsIsOrderedByIndexNotByPointerString` pins it.
    `checkLimits` sorts with the same comparator for one convention
    rather than two, though its three pointers carry no index.
    Restoring either string sort is red.

16. **The two trivia, both fixed rather than recorded.** Duplicate
    parameter keys were refused by nothing and were last-wins in every
    pass that reads them — the type a predicate is checked against, the
    default that is bound — so the document meant one thing and read as
    another; `checkQuery` now refuses a second declaration of a key in
    the same shape it already refuses a duplicate set name, naming the
    pointer that declared it first (`TestAParameterKeyIsDeclaredOnce`).
    And `Service.pool` was stored and never read, against correction 2's
    own rule that this file ships only what Task 4 uses: it is removed,
    since Task 11 adds a `*dbq.Queries` rather than a pool. `hub` stays —
    it cannot be rebuilt from anything else the service holds, and `New`'s
    contract is where it arrives.

**Decided here, for Tasks 6, 7 and 15:**

- **`ResolvedLeaf.Value` is now typed for every operator**, and the type
  is on the struct's doc comment: a value coerced to `Type` for the
  scalar operators, a `bool` for `exists` and `empty`, a `float64` for
  the length operators, and `Values` rather than `Value` for the list and
  pair operators. Task 6 binds it without guessing (this supersedes the
  "whatever the caller put there" the review found).
- **`Resolved.Params` holds declared defaults only**, each coerced, and
  the type now says so: a parameter declared without a default has no
  entry, and a run-time binding never passes through this pass.
- **A parameter still cannot feed an `enum` or a `list<text>` field, and
  now the refusal says why.** Widening the check to admit `text` against
  `enum` was considered and rejected: the option check lives in
  `coerceOperand`, and a value bound at run time never passes through it,
  so the enum misspelling this language refuses at save time would come
  back as an empty picture — the exact failure mode §3 calls the most
  expensive one. The message therefore states the language limitation
  ("a parameter is text, number or bool, so write the value out") rather
  than naming two types that disagree, which reads like the author's
  mistake. **Task 15's `views.run` tool description must say the same
  thing.** `TestAParameterCannotFeedAnEnumOrAListField` pins it, with a
  text parameter feeding `@name` as its control.

### Task 5: `internal/graph` — one bounded walk, two callers, no drift

**Files:**
- Create: `internal/graph/walk.go`
- Create: `internal/graph/walk_test.go`

This is the settlement of the spec's open question O8, second half. The
analysis spec's §7 requires "one walk, two callers, no drift" and names
`internal/analysis` as the owner; this spec's §4.2 specifies a compiler
that emits its own `WITH RECURSIVE`. Views lands first, so the walk goes
in **neither package**: `internal/graph`, on exactly the precedent
`internal/paging` set, whose package comment already argues why a copy of
a walk is a copy of its bugs.

What `internal/graph` owns: **the SQL primitive** — a project-filtered,
depth-bounded, visited-path-guarded recursive walk over `relations`,
emitted as SQL text plus bind arguments. What it does not own: policy.
This sub-project's policy is "which entities a step reached"; the
analysis engine's is "reachability under `all`/`any` gating over relation
traits". Two policies, one walk.

**Three things in the emitted SQL are load-bearing, and each has a test
that watches it fail:**

1. **`project_id = $1` on every table reference, including inside the
   recursive term.** An anchor-only filter seeds correctly and then lets
   the walk leave the project through any edge whose far side lives
   elsewhere. Today both relation endpoints carry `project_id` so such an
   edge should not exist — but the isolation invariant must not rest on
   another table's data being correct.
2. **The visited-path guard.** The core spec deliberately allows
   prerequisite cycles as design errors to be surfaced, so **a cycle is
   legal content** and the guard is a correctness requirement, not a
   defensive one: a walk without it hangs on exactly the games the
   analysis sub-project exists to help. `TestAWalkTerminatesOnACycle`
   asserts termination *and* the node set, because a walk that returned
   nothing would also terminate.
3. **`depth < $n` applied in the recursive term**, where it prunes, not
   in an outer `WHERE` that would materialise the whole walk first.

**And the shape this does not reuse.** `ListEntitiesRelatedTo`
(`internal/db/queries/metamodel.sql:518`) selects the far end of an edge
in the join condition, with two arms, and its comment says the arms are
exclusive — which is true **because `direction` is a scalar constant**, so
one arm is dead on every row. `direction: "any"` makes both arms live at
once, and then a self-loop, or a pair joined by an edge in each direction,
matches twice and the walk doubles at every level. So this emitter writes
**one arm per direction**, each computing the far endpoint as a scalar
`CASE`, and `direction: "any"` is the `UNION ALL` of the two arms under
one path guard rather than a two-armed `OR` inside one join.

- [ ] **Step 1: Write the failing tests**

`internal/graph/walk_test.go` (package `graph_test`; it seeds a tiny
graph with raw SQL because this package must not depend on
`internal/metamodel`):

```go
package graph_test

import (
	"context"
	"strings"
	"testing"

	"github.com/google/uuid"

	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/graph"
	"github.com/neverbot/maestro/internal/testutil"
)

// TestTheProjectFilterIsInBothTermsOfTheRecursion is the isolation
// assertion, and it is a *text* assertion on purpose: the behavioural
// version cannot fail while the composite foreign keys hold, which is
// exactly the reason the metamodel's own traversal comment gives for
// keeping a filter no test can catch. Asserting the emitted SQL is what
// makes the rule enforceable rather than aspirational.
func TestTheProjectFilterIsInBothTermsOfTheRecursion(t *testing.T) {
	sql, _ := graph.WalkCTE(graph.Walk{
		Name: "w", ProjectID: uuid.New(), SeedSQL: "SELECT id FROM entities WHERE false",
		RelationTypeIDs: []uuid.UUID{uuid.New()}, Direction: graph.Out, MinDepth: 1, MaxDepth: 3,
	})
	anchor, recursive, ok := strings.Cut(sql, "UNION ALL")
	if !ok {
		t.Fatalf("a bounded walk must be recursive:\n%s", sql)
	}
	for name, half := range map[string]string{"anchor": anchor, "recursive term": recursive} {
		if !strings.Contains(half, "project_id = $1") {
			t.Errorf("the %s does not filter on the project:\n%s", name, half)
		}
	}
}

func TestAWalkTerminatesOnACycle(t *testing.T) {
	ctx := context.Background()
	pool := testutil.NewPool(t)
	f := seedTriangleWithACycle(t, ctx, pool) // a -> b -> c -> a

	sql, args := graph.WalkCTE(graph.Walk{
		Name: "w", ProjectID: f.projectID,
		SeedSQL: "SELECT id FROM entities WHERE project_id = $1 AND id = $2",
		SeedArgs: []any{f.projectID, f.a}, RelationTypeIDs: []uuid.UUID{f.relTypeID},
		Direction: graph.Out, MinDepth: 1, MaxDepth: 10, MaxRows: 1000,
	})
	rows, err := pool.Query(ctx, "WITH RECURSIVE "+sql+" SELECT DISTINCT id FROM w", args...)
	if err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("the walk must run: %v\n%s", err, sql)
	}
	defer rows.Close()
	got := map[uuid.UUID]bool{}
	for rows.Next() {
		var id uuid.UUID
		if err := rows.Scan(&id); err != nil {
			t.Fatalf("scan: %v", err)
		}
		got[id] = true
	}
	// Termination alone would also be satisfied by an empty answer, which
	// is why the node set is asserted: a cycle is legal content and the
	// walk must return all of it, once.
	for name, id := range map[string]uuid.UUID{"a": f.a, "b": f.b, "c": f.c} {
		if !got[id] {
			t.Errorf("the walk must reach %s: a cycle is legal content, not a reason to stop early", name)
		}
	}
	if len(got) != 3 {
		t.Fatalf("the walk visited %d nodes and the graph has 3", len(got))
	}
}

func TestDepthBoundsTheWalk(t *testing.T) {
	ctx := context.Background()
	pool := testutil.NewPool(t)
	f := seedChain(t, ctx, pool, 5) // e0 -> e1 -> e2 -> e3 -> e4

	for _, tc := range []struct{ maxDepth, want int }{{1, 1}, {2, 2}, {4, 4}} {
		sql, args := graph.WalkCTE(graph.Walk{
			Name: "w", ProjectID: f.projectID,
			SeedSQL: "SELECT id FROM entities WHERE project_id = $1 AND id = $2",
			SeedArgs: []any{f.projectID, f.ids[0]}, RelationTypeIDs: []uuid.UUID{f.relTypeID},
			Direction: graph.Out, MinDepth: 1, MaxDepth: tc.maxDepth, MaxRows: 1000,
		})
		var n int
		if err := pool.QueryRow(ctx,
			"WITH RECURSIVE "+sql+" SELECT count(DISTINCT id) FROM w", args...).Scan(&n); err != nil {
			t.Fatalf("max_depth %d: %v", tc.maxDepth, err)
		}
		if n != tc.want {
			t.Errorf("max_depth %d reached %d entities, want %d", tc.maxDepth, n, tc.want)
		}
	}
}

// TestDirectionAnyDoesNotDoubleASymmetricEdge is the defect the shipped
// one-hop traversal's shape would have introduced here: two arms in one
// join condition are exclusive only while direction is a scalar
// constant, and "any" is where that stops being true.
func TestDirectionAnyDoesNotDoubleASymmetricEdge(t *testing.T) {
	ctx := context.Background()
	pool := testutil.NewPool(t)
	f := seedSymmetricPair(t, ctx, pool) // a -> b and b -> a, both of the same type

	sql, args := graph.WalkCTE(graph.Walk{
		Name: "w", ProjectID: f.projectID,
		SeedSQL: "SELECT id FROM entities WHERE project_id = $1 AND id = $2",
		SeedArgs: []any{f.projectID, f.a}, RelationTypeIDs: []uuid.UUID{f.relTypeID},
		Direction: graph.Any, MinDepth: 1, MaxDepth: 4, MaxRows: 1000,
	})
	var n int
	if err := pool.QueryRow(ctx,
		"WITH RECURSIVE "+sql+" SELECT count(*) FROM w WHERE depth > 0", args...).Scan(&n); err != nil {
		t.Fatalf("run: %v\n%s", err, sql)
	}
	if n != 1 {
		t.Fatalf("a symmetric pair must produce one reached row, got %d: the walk is "+
			"counting the same hop under both arms", n)
	}
}
```

The three fixtures (`seedTriangleWithACycle`, `seedChain`,
`seedSymmetricPair`) are plain SQL inserts in the same file, each
returning a struct with `projectID`, `relTypeID` and the entity ids; they
follow `internal/db/views_schema_test.go`'s `seedGame` exactly and are
written the same way.

- [ ] **Step 3: Write `internal/graph/walk.go`**

```go
// Package graph emits the one bounded traversal Maestro walks a game's
// relations with: project-filtered in both terms, depth-bounded, and
// guarded against revisiting a node on the path it arrived by.
//
// It exists in neither of its callers on purpose. The views sub-project's
// query language compiles traversal steps into it; the analysis engine
// will compile its reachability closure into it. Both specs asked for
// "one walk, two callers, no drift", neither said who owned it, and
// deciding after both were written is how the drift happens. The
// precedent is internal/paging, extracted for exactly this reason: a copy
// of a cursor is a copy of its bugs, and a copy of a walk is a copy of a
// missing project filter.
//
// **As of this commit there is one caller, internal/views.**
// internal/analysis does not exist yet. That is stated rather than
// implied, because a package comment claiming two callers it cannot point
// at is the "documentation claiming more than the code does" defect this
// repository has produced seventeen times.
//
// What lives here is the SQL primitive. Policy — which relation types
// gate what, whether a container propagates reachability, what a step's
// result means — belongs to the caller, and this package has no opinion
// about any of it.
package graph

import (
	"fmt"
	"strings"

	"github.com/google/uuid"
)

// Direction is which way an edge is followed, relative to the node the
// walk is standing on.
type Direction string

const (
	Out Direction = "out"
	In  Direction = "in"
	Any Direction = "any"
)

// Walk describes one bounded traversal.
//
// SeedSQL is a SELECT returning one uuid column, spliced in as the
// recursion's anchor. **It is the caller's own SQL and never a caller's
// text**: internal/views builds it from a compiled selector whose every
// value is already a bind parameter. This package cannot check that, so
// it is stated as the contract and pinned on the views side by
// TestNoCallerValueEverReachesTheStatementText (Task 6).
type Walk struct {
	// Name is the CTE's name. It must be a Go-side constant or a
	// generated identifier such as "s3" — never anything derived from a
	// caller's document. WalkCTE panics on a name that is not an
	// identifier, so a future caller that gets this wrong fails loudly at
	// its first test rather than quietly at somebody's database.
	Name string

	ProjectID       uuid.UUID
	SeedSQL         string
	SeedArgs        []any
	RelationTypeIDs []uuid.UUID
	Direction       Direction

	// MinDepth and MaxDepth bound the hops. Depth 0 is the seed itself,
	// which the CTE always carries because the guard needs it on the
	// path; MinDepth is applied by the *caller* when it reads the CTE, so
	// a step with min 2 still walks through depth 1.
	MinDepth int
	MaxDepth int

	// MaxRows caps the rows the recursion may build, so a pathological
	// branching factor cannot assemble a giant intermediate before an
	// outer LIMIT applies. Zero means no cap, which no caller in this
	// repository uses.
	MaxRows int
}

var identifier = func(s string) bool {
	if s == "" {
		return false
	}
	for i, r := range s {
		switch {
		case r >= 'a' && r <= 'z', r == '_':
		case i > 0 && r >= '0' && r <= '9':
		default:
			return false
		}
	}
	return true
}

// WalkCTE returns the body of one `WITH RECURSIVE` CTE and the bind
// arguments it uses, numbered from $1.
//
// The columns it produces are (id, depth, path, via_relation, from_id):
// the node reached, how many hops away, the ids on the path that reached
// it, the relation walked to get there (null at depth 0) and the node it
// came from (null at depth 0). A caller that wants the edges a walk
// traversed reads via_relation; a caller that wants only the nodes reads
// id and ignores the rest.
//
// **Three things in the emitted statement are load-bearing:**
//
//   - project_id = $1 appears on every table reference, in the anchor and
//     in the recursive term. TestTheProjectFilterIsInBothTermsOfTheRecursion
//     asserts it as text, because the behavioural version of that test
//     cannot fail while 0004_metamodel.sql's composite keys hold — and
//     the invariant must not rest on another table's data being correct.
//   - NOT (... = ANY(path)) is the cycle guard, and it is a correctness
//     requirement rather than a defensive one: the core spec deliberately
//     allows prerequisite cycles as design errors to be surfaced, so a
//     cycle is legal content and a walk without the guard hangs on
//     exactly the games this engine exists to help.
//     TestAWalkTerminatesOnACycle pins termination and the node set.
//   - depth < $n sits in the recursive term, where it prunes, and not in
//     an outer WHERE, which would materialise the whole walk first.
//
// The two directions are emitted as **two arms of the UNION ALL, one
// each**, rather than as a two-armed OR inside one join condition. The
// shipped one-hop traversal (internal/db/queries/metamodel.sql,
// ListEntitiesRelatedTo) uses the OR form and its comment says the arms
// are exclusive; they are, but only because its `direction` is a scalar
// constant, so one arm is dead on every row. Under Any both arms are
// live, and a self-loop or a pair joined in both directions matches twice
// and the walk doubles at every level.
// TestDirectionAnyDoesNotDoubleASymmetricEdge pins that it does not.
func WalkCTE(w Walk) (string, []any) {
	if !identifier(w.Name) {
		panic(fmt.Sprintf("graph: a CTE name must be a lower-case identifier, got %q; a name "+
			"derived from a caller's document is how this becomes an injection", w.Name))
	}
	args := []any{w.ProjectID}
	bind := func(v any) string {
		args = append(args, v)
		return fmt.Sprintf("$%d", len(args))
	}
	// The seed's own arguments are re-bound in order, so a caller can
	// build SeedSQL with its own numbering and have it renumbered here.
	seed := renumber(w.SeedSQL, len(args))
	args = append(args, w.SeedArgs...)

	types := bind(w.RelationTypeIDs)
	maxDepth := bind(w.MaxDepth)

	var arms []string
	for _, dir := range directionsOf(w.Direction) {
		near, far := "r.source_id", "r.target_id"
		if dir == In {
			near, far = "r.target_id", "r.source_id"
		}
		arms = append(arms, fmt.Sprintf(`
    SELECT %[2]s, w.depth + 1, w.path || %[2]s, r.id, w.id
    FROM %[1]s w
    JOIN relations r
      ON r.project_id = $1
     AND r.relation_type_id = ANY(%[3]s)
     AND %[4]s = w.id
    WHERE w.depth < %[5]s
      AND NOT %[2]s = ANY(w.path)`, w.Name, far, types, near, maxDepth))
	}

	body := fmt.Sprintf(`%[1]s (id, depth, path, via_relation, from_id) AS (
    SELECT seed.id, 0, ARRAY[seed.id], NULL::uuid, NULL::uuid
    FROM (%[2]s) AS seed
    JOIN entities anchor ON anchor.id = seed.id AND anchor.project_id = $1
  UNION ALL%[3]s
)`, w.Name, seed, strings.Join(arms, "\n  UNION ALL"))

	if w.MaxRows > 0 {
		// LIMIT inside a recursive CTE is not allowed in the recursive
		// term, so the cap is applied by wrapping the whole CTE in a
		// bounded select. It bounds the intermediate the caller then
		// reads, which is what the cap is for.
		body = fmt.Sprintf("%s, %s_capped AS (SELECT * FROM %s LIMIT %s)",
			body, w.Name, w.Name, bind(w.MaxRows))
	}
	return body, args
}

// CappedName is the CTE a caller should read from: the capped wrapper
// when MaxRows is set, the walk itself otherwise.
func CappedName(w Walk) string {
	if w.MaxRows > 0 {
		return w.Name + "_capped"
	}
	return w.Name
}

func directionsOf(d Direction) []Direction {
	if d == Any {
		return []Direction{Out, In}
	}
	return []Direction{d}
}

// renumber shifts a seed statement's $1..$n up by offset, so a caller can
// write its seed with its own numbering. It rewrites only $N tokens and
// leaves everything else alone; a seed containing a literal "$1" inside a
// string would be rewritten, which is why SeedSQL is documented as the
// caller's own SQL with no caller text in it.
func renumber(sql string, offset int) string {
	var b strings.Builder
	for i := 0; i < len(sql); i++ {
		if sql[i] != '$' {
			b.WriteByte(sql[i])
			continue
		}
		j := i + 1
		for j < len(sql) && sql[j] >= '0' && sql[j] <= '9' {
			j++
		}
		if j == i+1 {
			b.WriteByte('$')
			continue
		}
		n := 0
		for _, c := range sql[i+1 : j] {
			n = n*10 + int(c-'0')
		}
		fmt.Fprintf(&b, "$%d", n+offset)
		i = j - 1
	}
	return b.String()
}
```
